

### Observable-context RPS feasibility: early headroom, one no-message cell fails

- **Scope / implementation.** After the user's direction to evaluate a new task, ran only the separately locked, no-message feasibility stage above. The default game, `js/rps.js`, `js/sim.js`, connectome, and dopamine learning rule were unchanged. The opt-in runner used neutral context 0/1 VPN pulses alongside the existing previous-opponent-move VPN cue and eight own-VPN-routed Q heads. Seeds **951–953** were unused; previously used seeds **901–903** were excluded. No Fly A, DN message, teacher advice, or hidden-rule input was involved.
- **Validity.** All three students completed 12 fixed 19-round episodes and frozen disconnected evaluations at 0/1/3/12 episodes. Context and move VPN routes were exact with no ties; all training episodes had three samples per context×move cell; all held-out cells had 36 samples across three sequences. Selected-head updates matched the original `0.03(reward−Q)` equation, no nonselected head changed, KC→MBON multipliers stayed one, and frozen Q weights remained unchanged.
- **Results.** Episode-1 expected-optimal accuracy was **106/216 (49.1%)** for 951, **72/216 (33.3%)** for 952, and **68/216 (31.5%)** for 953; all passed the locked ≤75% early-headroom gate. At episode 12, 951 and 953 scored **216/216** each, while 952 scored **180/216**. Seed 952 failed only context 0 / previous-move cue 2: **0/36** held-out optimal choices. Its 36 training visits were routed correctly but played Rock **35** times, Scissors once, and Paper zero times; it received **zero** rewards on that cell, where Paper is optimal. Thus the failure is action/reward starvation, not sensory routing. The other five cells for 952 scored **36/36** each.
- **Decision / limits.** The all-student eventual-learnability gate failed, so this task has **not** passed the prerequisite for a teacher-source or teacher-student experiment. The failed cell is a plausible target for advice because the student never sampled its rewarding action, while the other two students learned all cells; however, no result shows Fly A can identify or correct it, and no teacher benefit is established. Stop this direction under its locked gate: do not extend the budget, change action exploration, or start communication based on this held-out failure. Any revised task/controller protocol requires a new prospective design and authorization. Prior experiments and their gates remain unchanged.
- **Artifact / reproduction.** Write-once `data/rps_context_cued_feasibility.json` contains the exact protocol, per-round inputs/actions/rewards, all checkpoints, and gates. `node --check tools/check_rps_context_cued_feasibility.mjs` passed; reproduce in a copy without the artifact using `node tools/check_rps_context_cued_feasibility.mjs` (overwrite refused). No teacher training or additional simulation followed.

# Synapse: technical notes and lab notebook

> Historical notes for the original rhythm-game experiment. The current RPS experiment and its measured data are described in [README](../README.md) and `data/rps_results.json`.

The full detail behind the [README](../README.md): what is simulated, the learning rules, every control, complete results with spreads, and what I tried along the way.

## The model moves to Python

The model, experiments, training and tests are now Python (`flyhero/`, NumPy only). The page still needs the model to run live in the browser, so `js/sim.js`, `js/game.js` and `js/agents.js` are a line-for-line port. Every number below comes from the Python code.

**Same spikes in both languages.** Both versions use float64 throughout and the same random number generator (mulberry32), consume random numbers in the same order, and add synaptic input in the same order, so rounding matches too. The two decay constants, exp(-1/5) and exp(-1/200), are written out as numbers because library exp functions can differ in the last digit. `python -m flyhero.test` runs Bio-RL (real wiring, 2 songs), Deep-RL (real wiring, 2 songs) and Bio-RL on shuffled and random wiring (1 song each) in both languages and checks that every spike and key press is identical. Learned weights agree to within 1e-9; tanh, exp and log1p differ in the last digit between Node and NumPy, which never changed a spike or a press.

**The check found a bug in the old JavaScript.** Bio-RL's eligibility snapshot, the Kenyon cell spike traces used to decide which synapses change, was built with `.map` on an integer array, which returns an integer array. Traces were rounded down to whole numbers, so a synapse whose Kenyon cell fired recently but had a trace below 1 did not change at all. The Python model uses the real values, and the JavaScript port is fixed. The old version also stored weights, traces and the Deep-RL readout as float32, so every condition was rerun.

**Speed.** NumPy runs one song in about 5 to 14 seconds, against about 0.5 seconds for the JavaScript, because each millisecond is a separate set of array operations. The 38 runs go in parallel, one process each: 3,218 seconds (54 minutes) on 22 cores. The You vs fly opponent (Bio-RL, real wiring, seed 1) goes from 28% to 100% on the practice song after 30 songs, and the browser port scores 100% with the saved weights.

**Results from the Python model** (5 repeats of 30 songs; 3 repeats of 150 songs for the last row; mean and spread across repeats):

| Learner | Before practice | Song 1 | End of practice | Runs reaching 80% (song) | New song | Presses on empty beats (last song) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Bio-RL, real wiring | 33.1% | 61.9% | 99.3% ± 0.5 | 5/5 (4, 3, 4, 3, 3) | 100.0% ± 0.0 | 0.0 / 16 |
| Bio-RL, shuffled wiring | 21.9% | 21.9% | 21.9% ± 0.0 | 0/5 | 27.2% ± 1.3 | 15.0 / 16 |
| Bio-RL, random network | 15.0% | 23.1% | 25.9% ± 14.2 | 0/5 | 26.2% ± 13.7 | 0.0 / 16 |
| Bio-RL, dopamine blocked | 31.9% | 36.3% | 33.3% ± 2.6 | 0/5 | 35.9% ± 5.4 | 0.0 / 16 |
| Deep-RL, real wiring | 23.1% | 50.6% | 99.0% ± 0.6 | 5/5 (5, 4, 4, 4, 5) | 97.4% ± 1.6 | 0.6 / 16 |
| Deep-RL, shuffled wiring | 23.1% | 30.6% | 82.8% ± 11.6 | 4/5 (10, 11, 29, 6) | 75.9% ± 15.9 | 9.6 / 16 |
| Deep-RL, random network | 23.1% | 78.1% | 100.0% ± 0.0 | 5/5 (3, 3, 3, 3, 3) | 100.0% ± 0.0 | 0.0 / 16 |
| Deep-RL, real wiring, 150 songs | 21.9% | 47.9% | 98.5% ± 1.6 | 3/3 (5, 4, 4) | 95.7% ± 1.2 | 0.0 / 16 |

**The conclusions hold.** Bio-RL on the real wiring still learns (99.3%, 100% on the new song) and now passes 80% a little sooner (song 3 or 4 on every run, against 3 to 5). It still fails on shuffled (21.9%) and random (25.9%) wiring and with dopamine blocked (33.3%). Deep-RL still learns on every wiring, and the two learners are still tied on the real wiring (99.3% and 99.0%). The rest of this notebook, including the learning rate sweep, was measured with the JavaScript version.

## Switch to the Janelia and Google male CNS

The project was meant to use the Janelia and Google male CNS connectome from the start. Its neuPrint service needs a personal login token, which I couldn't get during the unattended first build, so the first version used the public FlyWire (female brain) files instead and borrowed male CNS cell positions only for the drawing. Janelia also publishes the male CNS as plain file downloads with no login, so the whole project now uses it: wiring, synapse signs (from its neurotransmitter predictions), the drawing, and each neuron's own position.

**New network.** Same selection rules, now from the male CNS: 62 eye neurons, 2,045 Kenyon cells, 1 APL, 42 output neurons, 158 reward and 8 punishment dopamine neurons, 461 intermediate and 498 descending neurons. That is 3,275 neurons, 518,428 connections and 1,922,033 synapses; 12 neurons have no recorded cell body and are not drawn. Synapse signs come from the male CNS neurotransmitter predictions, using the same rule as the Shiu model (GABA, glutamate and histamine inhibit, everything else excites).

**No boost needed.** Driving one lane's eye neurons at the plain model weights already makes all three output groups fire (about 1 to 2 spikes per neuron). The old boost of 8 now overdrives the network: Kenyon cell spikes jump from about 80 to about 2,300 and reward dopamine neurons fire with no reward. The boost is now 1.

**Bio-RL, seed 1, boost 1:** 53%, 75%, 94%, then 97 to 100% from song 4; 100% on the new song. With dopamine blocked it stays at 28 to 38% (33% on the new song).

**Deep-RL first failed, and the reason was the readout, not the brain.** With the old setup it sat at about 41% for 30 songs at every learning rate and pressed on almost every empty beat. To check whether the descending neurons carry lane information, I recorded their spike counts for 288 beats with learning off and trained a plain readout that is told the answer: 100% accuracy on held-out beats (the most common answer alone gives 30%), and 98% from the output neurons. So the information is there; hundreds of active inputs with large counts were swamping the softmax. Deep-RL now standardizes each input with running statistics, a standard step for this kind of readout.

**Deep-RL learning rate sweep on the male CNS** (seed 1, 30 songs, standardized inputs):

| Learning rate | Mean of songs 26 to 30 | New song |
|---|---|---|
| 0.03 | 99% | 97% |
| 0.01 | 98% | 95% |
| 0.003 | 96% | 95% |
| 0.001 | 91% | 90% |

I picked 0.03, the highest mean of songs 26 to 30, using the same rule as the original sweep.

**Male CNS results, JavaScript version before the eligibility fix** (5 repeats of 30 songs; 3 repeats of 150 songs for the last row; mean and spread across repeats):

| Learner | Before practice | Song 1 | End of practice | Runs reaching 80% (song) | New song | Presses on empty beats (last song) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Bio-RL, real wiring | 31.9% | 61.9% | 99.6% ± 0.3 | 5/5 (4, 5, 5, 3, 3) | 100.0% ± 0.0 | 0.0 / 16 |
| Bio-RL, shuffled wiring | 21.9% | 21.9% | 21.9% ± 0.0 | 0/5 | 26.7% ± 1.3 | 15.0 / 16 |
| Bio-RL, random network | 15.0% | 21.9% | 26.3% ± 14.3 | 0/5 | 25.6% ± 13.3 | 0.0 / 16 |
| Bio-RL, dopamine blocked | 30.0% | 32.5% | 35.3% ± 2.5 | 0/5 | 37.9% ± 5.5 | 0.0 / 16 |
| Deep-RL, real wiring | 23.1% | 49.4% | 98.9% ± 0.8 | 5/5 (5, 4, 4, 4, 4) | 97.9% ± 2.5 | 0.4 / 16 |
| Deep-RL, shuffled wiring | 23.1% | 29.4% | 80.0% ± 11.6 | 2/5 (19, 6) | 72.8% ± 6.2 | 6.0 / 16 |
| Deep-RL, random network | 23.1% | 78.1% | 100.0% ± 0.0 | 5/5 (3, 3, 3, 3, 3) | 100.0% ± 0.0 | 0.0 / 16 |
| Deep-RL, real wiring, 150 songs | 21.9% | 46.9% | 99.8% ± 0.3 | 3/3 (5, 4, 4) | 97.4% ± 2.1 | 0.3 / 16 |

**Why the brain-style learner fails on scrambled wiring.** I drove one lane's eye neurons at a time and counted spikes. In the real wiring each output group fires about 1 to 2 spikes per neuron and Kenyon cells stay sparse. In shuffled wiring, activity snowballs after the first input: intermediate and descending neurons fire thousands of spikes and output neurons up to 15 per neuron, so the fly presses the same lane on almost every beat (15 of 16 empty beats on the last song) and learning never gets started. In a random network the eyes' signal barely reaches the output neurons (0 to 1.4 spikes per neuron), so there is nothing to strengthen. Descending neurons still carry some lane-specific activity there, which is why the trained readout learns on a random network.

**This reverses the FlyWire result.** On FlyWire, shuffled wiring did as well as the real wiring for Bio-RL (100% vs 96%). On the male CNS, the real wiring's balance is what lets learning inside the brain work.

**Punishment dopamine fires a little on its own.** Over 5 songs with learning on, the 8 PPL1 neurons fired about 4 spikes in the 100 ms after a hit (in 66% of hits), about 73 after a mistake, and about 15 during the decision window before any feedback, all from ordinary network input. Reward dopamine (PAM) fired about 315 spikes after a hit. The stray punishment spikes are small next to the real signals, so learning still works.

**Nerve cord added.** 3,625 nerve cord neurons that get at least 100 synapses from the simulated descending neurons, plus the connections among them: 6,900 neurons, 990,102 connections and 6,419,481 synapses in total. Cord to brain connections are dropped and the wiring controls leave the cord untouched. Replaying Bio-RL on real, shuffled and random wiring, and Deep-RL on real wiring, with and without the cord gave identical key presses. With real wiring, 2,153 of the 3,625 cord neurons fire. The page still plays at 1.00× speed.

**The sections below describe the original FlyWire version.** Results from the male CNS rerun are recorded here once it finishes.

## What is simulated

This is the right-hemisphere mushroom-body loop, selected by fixed rules in `tools/extract_subgraph.py`:

| Role | Rule | Neurons |
|---|---|---|
| eyes | visual projection neurons with ≥10 synapses onto right Kenyon cells, dealt round-robin into 3 lanes | 58 |
| memory | all right Kenyon cells (KCs) | 2,597 |
| inhibition | the right APL neuron | 1 |
| output | MBONs with ≥500 synapses from right KCs, dealt round-robin into 3 action groups | 35 |
| reward / punishment | right PAM / PPL1 dopamine neurons | 153 / 8 |
| downstream | neurons with ≥10 synapses from an MBON and ≥10 onto a descending neuron | 206 |
| descending | descending neurons with ≥10 synapses from an MBON or downstream neuron | 313 |

Every connectome edge between these neurons is kept: **3,371 neurons, 233,686 connections, 650,482 synapses**.

The neuron model is Shiu et al. 2024's LIF: v_rest −52 mV, threshold −45 mV, τ_m 20 ms, τ_syn 5 ms, 0.275 mV per synapse (signed by neurotransmitter), and 150 Hz Poisson input for driven neurons. This project runs it at **dt = 1 ms**, coarser than the original, with delay and refractory period rounded to 2 ms.

The brain panel shows the cell bodies of 139,662 neurons from the Janelia male CNS, a fly whose brain and nerve cord were imaged together. It is seen from above with the head at the top, so the nerve cord runs down the page, as it runs back along a real fly's body. FlyWire covers the brain only, which is why the drawing uses the male CNS.

The simulated neurons come from FlyWire. Each is drawn at the cell body of a male CNS neuron of the same type, on the same side where possible; 26 of the 3,371 have no matching type and aren't drawn. Only simulated neurons ever flash, in the ink colour, with green for reward dopamine (PAM) and magenta for punishment dopamine (PPL1).

## The RL problem, stated honestly

| | |
|---|---|
| Song | 48 slots, 400 ms apart. Each slot is a note in lane 1-3 or a rest (16 of 48 are rests). |
| State | What the fly's eyes see as a slot approaches: the visual neurons for that lane get 150 Hz input from 200 ms before the hit line. |
| Actions | Press lane 1, 2 or 3, or wait. The press registers 40 ms after the hit line, inside the ±150 ms window humans get. |
| Reward | +1 hit, −1 wrong lane, −1 miss, −0.5 press on a rest, 0 wait on a rest |
| Episode | One play of the song |

Actions don't change what comes next, so this is a **contextual bandit**, not a multi-step problem. Agent A's rule is reward-prediction-error learning with eligibility traces. It is *not* temporal-difference learning across time steps, and nothing here claims it is. Because the state is "which lane", the fly can be tested on a song it never trained on.

### Agent A: Bio-RL (learning inside the connectome)

1. **Choosing.** Lane input drives visual neurons, then Kenyon cells, then MBONs. The action group with the most MBON spikes per neuron in the decision window wins, if it reaches 0.5 spikes per neuron; otherwise the fly waits. Exploration comes only from spiking noise (subthreshold Poisson input on MBONs).
2. **Prediction and error.** Prediction V = tanh(winner spikes / 2). Error δ = reward − V.
3. **Dopamine is simulated.** PAM neurons are driven in proportion to max(δ, 0) and PPL1 neurons to max(−δ, 0), for 100 ms. Their spike counts give the dopamine signal D.
4. **Update.** Only KC→MBON connections onto the chosen group change: m ← clip(m + 0.15 · D · KC trace, 0, 4), where the KC spike trace has τ = 200 ms.
5. **No press, no change.** A wait has no eligible synapses.

This is built to avoid a common problem with simple dopamine learning rules, where punishments wear down the useful synapses:
- The error shrinks to zero once a pathway predicts its reward.
- Only the chosen pathway and recently active KCs are eligible.
- The APL keeps KC codes sparse (about 20 of 2,597 KCs fire per lane).
- Weights are bounded.
- Misses change nothing.

### Agent B: Deep-RL (frozen connectome + trained readout)

Plasticity is off and the dopamine neurons get no input. A linear softmax policy reads log(1 + spike count) from the 313 descending neurons and is trained with REINFORCE and a running reward baseline, learning rate 0.002. PPO would add nothing on a one-step problem.

### Controls

| Control | What changes |
|---|---|
| shuffled | Within each role-to-role block (e.g. KC→MBON), postsynaptic partners are permuted. Every neuron keeps its per-block in- and out-degree and its synapse counts, and the architecture stays intact. |
| random | A generic network: same neurons, same number of connections, same weights, but random endpoints. |
| dopamine blocked | Agent A with the PAM and PPL1 drive set to zero. |

All the numbers above were set once, before the full experiment runs. They are recorded in `flyhero/agents.py` and in the lab notebook below.

## Results

Setup: 5 seeds × 30 training episodes on "Banana Drift" (32 notes, 16 rests), then one frozen play of the unseen song "Wing Hum". The raw console output is in `notes/experiments.log`; full curves are in `data/results.json`.

- **Before** is a frozen play before any learning.
- **Last 5** is the mean hit % over episodes 26-30.
- **Seeds ≥80%** counts seeds whose 3-episode moving average reached 80%.

All values are mean ± sd across seeds, as % of notes hit.

| Condition | Before | Episode 1 | Last 5 | Seeds ≥80% (episode reached) | Unseen song | False presses on rests (last episode) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **A-real** (Bio-RL) | 40.0 | 66.9 | **96.0 ± 0.3** | 5/5 (3, 3, 4, 4, 3) | **96.4 ± 4.5** | 0 / 16 |
| A-shuffled | 38.1 | 76.3 | 100.0 ± 0.0 | 5/5 (3, 3, 3, 3, 3) | 100.0 ± 0.0 | 0 / 16 |
| A-random | 5.6 | 7.5 | 6.6 ± 13.3 | 0/5 | 9.2 ± 18.5 | 0 / 16 |
| A-dopamine-blocked | 39.4 | 39.4 | 41.0 ± 1.2 | 0/5 | 35.9 ± 2.8 | 0 / 16 |
| B-real (Deep-RL) | 23.1 | 26.9 | 56.3 ± 10.2 | 0/5 | 56.9 ± 12.1 | 11.2 / 16 |
| B-shuffled | 23.1 | 24.4 | 73.6 ± 2.3 | 2/5 (28, 30) | 67.2 ± 6.8 | 12.8 / 16 |
| B-random | 23.1 | 23.1 | 47.5 ± 15.5 | 0/5 | 43.6 ± 17.1 | 13.0 / 16 |
| B-real, 150 episodes (3 seeds) | 21.9 | 30.2 | 74.8 ± 0.6 | 0/3 | 68.4 ± 1.2 | 8.0 / 16 |

For the 150-episode row, "Last 5" means episodes 146-150.

### What the controls support

**Headline (supported).** The Bio-RL fly goes from 40% to 96% of notes hit in 30 plays. Every seed passes 80% by play 3-4. It scores 96% on a song it never trained on. Blocking its dopamine neurons keeps it at 41%.

The learning happens inside the simulated connectome, through dopamine-gated KC→MBON plasticity, with no trained readout.

**Real wiring does not beat shuffled wiring.** Randomly re-pairing partners within each cell-class block did as well or better, for both agents: A-shuffled 100% vs A-real 96%, and B-shuffled 74% vs B-real 56%. Nothing here shows that the specific FlyWire wiring matters for this task.

This fits Caron et al. 2013, who found KC inputs are close to random. One possible reason shuffled did slightly *better* (untested): the shuffle spreads visual input across more KCs and MBONs, which would remove the "group 3 barely hears the eyes" imbalance in the real wiring.

**The architecture does matter.** A generic random network with the same neurons, connection count and weights fails for Agent A (7%). There, visual input doesn't reach the output neurons in a usable way.

So the honest claim: *eyes → sparse Kenyon-cell expansion → MBONs, with dopamine-gated plasticity* does the work, not the exact synapse list.

**Bio-RL vs Deep-RL.** On the same 30-episode budget, dopamine plasticity inside the brain beat a REINFORCE readout on descending neurons in every wiring condition: 96 vs 56 (real), 100 vs 74 (shuffled), 7 vs 48 (random).

This comparison has caveats:
- **Rests aren't a fair fight.** Agent A's "wait on a rest" is built in (no visual input, no MBON spikes), while Agent B has to learn it and still false-presses on most rests.
- **Agent B is barely tuned.** It's a linear policy with one learning-rate sweep, reading neurons two synapses downstream of the mushroom body. A better-tuned readout could do better.
- **The random-network result reverses.** Agent B beats Agent A there (48% vs 7%). A readout can pull *some* lane signal out of arbitrary spiking; plasticity confined to KC→MBON synapses can't, if the eyes don't reach those synapses.

**More training doesn't close the gap.** With 5× the budget (150 episodes, 3 seeds), Deep-RL on real wiring levels off at 74.8% ± 0.6 and scores 68.4% on the unseen song. That's still well below the 96% Bio-RL reaches in 30 episodes.

**Where the 40% starting point comes from.** Before learning, MBON groups 1 and 2 respond to every lane and group 3 barely responds. The untrained fly therefore mostly presses lanes 1-2, which lands about 40% of notes. That's a structural bias, not skill, and it's why the blocked-dopamine control sits at the same level.

## Known biological liberties

1. **Signed plasticity.** Real dopamine at KC→MBON synapses mostly *depresses* them. Here reward strengthens the chosen pathway.
2. **Broadcast dopamine.** All PAM or all PPL1 neurons are driven together, not compartment by compartment.
3. **Prediction computed in code.** The prediction V is computed from MBON spikes by the learner's code, standing in for MBON→dopamine feedback (roughly as in Bennett et al. 2021).
4. **Winner-take-all.** The action readout over 3 MBON groups is a modeling choice. The groups are dealt round-robin by KC input, fixed before any results were seen.
5. **KC→MBON gain of 8.** Without it the MBONs never fire from visual input (see the lab notebook).
6. **Visual input.** Lanes are mapped onto real visual projection neurons that synapse onto KCs. That visual input is a small minority of what the mushroom body receives (about 6.5k synapses versus about 427k from central neurons, mostly olfactory).
7. **Drawing positions come from a different fly.** Simulated FlyWire neurons (female brain) are drawn at the cell bodies of same-type neurons in the Janelia male CNS, so flash positions are approximate, and they mark cell bodies rather than the synapse-dense regions where neurons connect.

## Lab notebook

**Data.** I wanted the male CNS connectome, but neuPrint needs an auth token, so I used the public FlyWire v783 files. The annotations have positions for all 139,248 neurons. Right-hemisphere neurons have the larger x values, so x is mirrored for a front view.

**First activity probe** (one lane's visual neurons at 150 Hz for 240 ms):
- About 20 visual neurons and 17-25 KCs fire. The code is sparse, and the APL fires a few spikes.
- **At the plain Shiu weights the MBONs were completely silent**, so nothing could learn.
- A KC→MBON gain of 4 gave about 3 spikes per MBON in groups 1-2 but under 0.4 in group 3. I picked gain 8.
- The round-robin MBON grouping left group 3 nearly deaf to vision, because the visually driven KCs talk to a handful of MBONs. I kept the grouping rather than re-pick it after seeing the data; learning has to strengthen group 3's weak pathway.
- Speed: about 0.45 s per 19.4 s song in Node, about 0.7 s in the browser.

**Agent A calibration.** The first parameter set learned on the first try. Seed 1 went from 66% in episode 1 to 97%, and scored 90% on the unseen song; seed 2 behaved similarly. No further tuning was done.
- Episode 1 is already about 66% because learning happens *during* the first play. That's why the runner also records a frozen "before" episode.
- Agent A never presses on rests. Without visual input, noise never reaches the press threshold, so "wait on a rest" is built in, not learned.

**Agent B learning-rate sweep** (seed 1, 30 episodes):

| Learning rate | Result |
|---|---|
| 0.1 | Collapsed straight to always pressing one lane (41% flat). |
| 0.01 | Flat for about 20 episodes, then climbed to 63%. |
| 0.005 | About 40%, then 53% at episode 30. |
| 0.002 | 28% rising to about 60%, still improving. |

I picked 0.002 because it was the steadiest; a single-seed sweep is noisy. Agent B keeps pressing on 8-12 of the 16 rests, because it samples from a softmax.

**Browser check.** Turbo looked stuck in one screenshot. Measured properly, it runs about 0.6 episodes per second; the screenshot had simply caught it right after a restart.

**Nerve cord added.** I couldn't find a brain stem in the drawing. Flies don't have one; the equivalent is the neck connective and the ventral nerve cord, and FlyWire's brain-only data leaves them out. The Janelia male CNS annotations are public, carry a soma position for each neuron, and include FlyWire cell types, so the brain panel now draws that fly's whole CNS from above. 3,345 of the 3,371 simulated neurons are placed at a cell of the same type.

**UI simplified.** The first page was a dark, neon-style dashboard: WebGL glow, labeled brain regions, a schematic nerve cord, a story strip and sound. It was too busy. It's now a plain white page with native controls, a black-on-white game, and a grey-dot brain on a 2D canvas. An accessibility pass followed: AA text contrast, visible keyboard focus, labelled canvases, dashed lines for Deep-RL, and 44px touch targets. A final design pass gave it a microscopy look: ink on white, GFP green and magenta for reward and punishment dopamine (the colour-blind-safe pair microscopists use), one legible typeface, and a figure-legend paragraph under the brain. The last cleanup pass:

- **Font is self-hosted.** The page no longer needs the internet.
- **Dark mode follows the system setting.** The canvases redraw from the same colour tokens.
- **The chart uses shade and dashes instead of extra colours.** Shade shows the wiring and dashes show the learner.
- **A missing-data message** now explains how to serve the folder. The simulation and experiments are unchanged.

## Two-bit channel gate

- **Iteration 1 — active bit-0 validation.** Hypothesis: stimulating KCs 1319/2016 for 2146 bit 0 generalizes without disrupting a learned cue rule. Existing independent Fly B seeds 518/519 scored 21/24 and 23/24 after training (disconnected 13/24 and 11/24; shuffled 13/24 and 12/24). A frozen cue-trained state scored 20/24 cue only, 21/24 with natural messages, and 21/24 shuffled. Gate passed preliminarily; rare message patterns remain under-sampled.
- **Iteration 2 — conditional two-bit decoding.** Hypothesis: Fly B can use bit 2146 for cue 0 and bit 2134 for cue 1, avoiding XOR. Fixed active channel, eight ordinary training episodes, 24 balanced held-out rounds per seed. Fly B seeds 517/518/519 scored 17/21/14 after training versus 16/13/12 disconnected and 15/12/10 shuffled; before training 16/10/11. Cue 1 with message `10` scored 1/9 combined. Gate failed; no recorded-teacher teaching experiment was run.
- **Iteration 3 — frozen cue-1 `10` mechanism.** Hypothesis: the irrelevant 2146 bit biases the action-2 MBON readout when 2134 bit 0 requires action 0. Reconstructed the *same* trained weights for Fly B seeds 518/519, checked that ordinary frozen scores match the saved 21/24 and 14/24, then changed only `10` to `00` on each of three cue-1 held-out rounds in a paired frozen replay. Earlier rounds and reward labels stayed identical; no learning occurred. Correct action-0 choices rose from 1/6 to 4/6. Mean message MBON spikes (action 0/2) changed from 0.5/3.5 to 7.67/2.0; decision readout from 0.24/0.70 to 0.69/0.54. Action-0 eligibility was present even in `10` (78.6 versus 54.0 after the flip). During training, cue-1 `10` received only 2/24 and 1/24 rewarded action-0 updates in seeds 518/519. The irrelevant-bit readout bias is a contributor, but two flipped rounds still failed. No teaching run or channel tuning followed.
- **Next experiment:** if pursuing this channel, pre-register a single interference-reducing interface on calibration seeds and test it on fresh teachers and students before any teaching claim. Do not choose it from these held-out rounds.

Reproduce iteration 2: `node tools/two_cue_transfer.mjs decode_two_bit_active0_conditional SEED 02 lanes02 150` with `SEED` 17, 18, or 19. Raw results: `data/two_cue_decode_two_bit_active0_conditional_02_{17,18,19}_lanes02.json`. Iteration 1 commands and raw files are recorded with the earlier results.

Reproduce iteration 3: `node tools/two_cue_transfer.mjs decode_two_bit_active0_probe SEED 02 lanes02 150` with `SEED` 18 or 19. Raw paired neural and training measurements: `data/two_cue_decode_two_bit_active0_probe_02_{18,19}_lanes02.json`.

### Cue-gated two-bit interference diagnostic

- **Research question / hypothesis.** Does stimulating the irrelevant 2146 bit during cue 1 cause the `10` decoding failure? If so, routing only the cue-relevant bit should improve cue-1 `10` decisions, compared with the original two-bit channel on the same trial schedule.
- **Experiment.** One paired Fly B seed (520); original active-zero channel versus a cue-gated interface that stimulates only 2146 for cue 0 or 2134 for cue 1. The gate is an engineered interface diagnostic, not a decoder installed in Fly B. Each arm used 8 ordinary 24-round training episodes with recorded teacher samples from seeds 501/502, then 24 balanced frozen held-out rounds from teacher seeds 503/504. Same student seed, messages, reward rule, and train/evaluation schedules in both arms. No connectome, dynamics, dopamine, or readout change.
- **Controls / measurements.** Original channel: 12/24 before, 18/24 after; after-training disconnected 15/24, cue-matched shuffled 12/24. Cue-gated: 9/24 before, 20/24 after; disconnected 14/24, shuffled 11/24. Cue 1 / `10` improved from 0/3 to 2/3; cue 1 / `00` worsened from 2/3 to 0/3. Cue 0 / `01` improved from 1/3 to 3/3. The original and gated arms used identical held-out messages and shuffles.
- **Conclusion / limitation.** Suppressing irrelevant-bit stimulation helped the suspected `10` pattern in this seed but did not make conditional decoding reliable. The lost `00` response suggests the active 2146-zero pathway was supplying useful action-0 drive even on cue 1; this mechanism remains unmeasured here. Single student seed, reused recorded teacher cohorts, and an engineered cue-aware gate do not establish teaching or generalization. The full-task gate failed, so no teacher-student teaching run followed.
- **Next justified experiment.** On calibration teacher recordings, compare cue-1 `00` and `10` message-window MBON action-0/2 spikes, decision rates, and action-0 eligibility under both interfaces at frozen weights. Test whether cue-gating removes useful action-0 drive for `00` before selecting any new target or intensity.
- **Reproduction / raw data.** `node tools/two_cue_transfer.mjs decode_two_bit_cue_gated_pair 20 02 lanes02 150`; `data/two_cue_decode_two_bit_cue_gated_pair_02_20_lanes02.json`.

### Frozen cue-gating pathway crossover

- **Question / hypothesis.** Does cue-gating fail on cue-1 `00` because it removes useful activity from the active 2146-zero channel? At fixed trained Fly B weights, removing that stimulation should lower action-0 MBON activity and action-0 choices for `00`, while relieving the competing action-2 drive for `10`.
- **Experiment / controls.** Reconstructed both seed-520 students from the prior paired experiment: original and cue-gated interfaces, each trained for eight 24-round episodes on recorded teachers 501/502. Both exactly reproduced saved training and frozen results. For each trained weight state, replayed the same 24 balanced held-out rounds from teachers 503/504 twice, changing only whether cue gating was enabled. Weights were frozen and verified unchanged; cue/message schedule and reward labels were fixed. Existing disconnected and cue-matched shuffled evaluations remained 15/24 and 12/24 for original training, 14/24 and 11/24 for gated training.
- **Measurements.** For cue-1 `00` (three rounds per weight state), original stimulation recruited the 2146-zero target KCs at a mean 13.33/15.33 spikes, produced mean message-window action-0 MBON counts 6.00/8.33, and selected action 0 on 2/3 and 2/3 rounds. Gating reduced target KC and action-0 MBON message spikes to zero; action-0 choices became 1/3 and 0/3. Mean decision action-0 rates fell from 0.524/0.690 to 0.238/0.095; action-0 eligibility sums fell from 47.12/53.20 to 30.05/26.00. For cue-1 `10`, original versus gated action-0 choices were 0/3 versus 2/3 in **both** trained states; mean message action-2 MBON counts fell from 2.33/3.33 to 0.33/0.33. Full-task cross-interface scores were 18/24 to 21/24 for original-trained weights and 20/24 to 17/24 for gated-trained weights.
- **Conclusion / limits.** The lost 2146-zero stimulation explains much of the `00` loss in this seed, while its bit-1 stimulation contributes to the `10` action-2 bias. This is a frozen channel mechanism diagnostic, not evidence of learning from a teacher. The full-schedule crossover can carry neural state effects from earlier rounds, each pattern has only three trials per weight state, and the same teacher cohorts and student seed were reused. Joint decoding remains inconsistent; no channel change or teaching experiment followed.
- **Next justified experiment.** Repeat this *fixed* pathway crossover on one fresh Fly B seed with the same teacher cohorts and pre-specified cue-1 `00`/`10` neural and choice endpoints. Stop if the two-direction pattern does not replicate; do not tune target neurons or intensity on these held-out rounds.
- **Reproduce / raw data.** `node tools/two_cue_transfer.mjs decode_two_bit_cue_gated_probe 20 02 lanes02 150`; [paired pathway measurements](../data/two_cue_decode_two_bit_cue_gated_probe_02_20_lanes02.json). The runner refuses to overwrite an existing result.

### Fresh-seed frozen cue-gating crossover

- **Question / hypothesis.** Would the seed-520 two-direction cue-1 pattern replicate at fixed trained weights in a new student: gating removes useful 2146-zero action-0 drive for `00` but removes competing 2146-one action-2 drive for `10`?
- **Experiment / controls.** Ran the existing `decode_two_bit_cue_gated_probe` unchanged with Fly B seed 521. Original and cue-gated students each received eight 24-round training episodes from recorded teacher seeds 501/502. Each trained weight state was frozen and crossed over both interfaces on the same 24 balanced held-out rounds from teachers 503/504; each cue-1 pattern had three rounds per state. The runner verified frozen weights. After-training original/gated scores were 17/24 and 19/24, versus disconnected 11/24 and 14/24 and cue-matched shuffled 12/24 and 15/24.
- **Measurements.** On cue-1 `00`, original-trained weights changed from 3/3 action-0 choices with the original interface to 2/3 gated; gated-trained weights changed from 2/3 original to 1/3 gated. Mean message-window action-0 MBON spikes fell 7.33→0 and 7.67→0; active 2146-zero target KC spikes fell 15.33→0 in both states. Mean action-0 decision rates fell 0.643→0.381 and 0.595→0.167; action-0 eligibility fell 55.23→34.39 and 53.47→26.86. On cue-1 `10`, action-2 message spikes fell 4.00→0.33 and 4.33→1.00 with gating, but action-0 choices changed 0/3→1/3 in original-trained weights and 1/3→0/3 in gated-trained weights.
- **Conclusion / limitations.** The neural drive and `00` choice losses replicated. The proposed `10` choice rescue did **not** replicate across both trained states, so the predeclared two-direction gate failed. This is a frozen interface mechanism probe, not a teacher-student learning or teaching test. Three trials per pattern, a single new student seed, reused teacher cohorts, and within-schedule neural state effects limit inference.
- **Next experiment.** Stop this fixed-channel replication line as predeclared. A new prospective interface hypothesis would need independent calibration and a fresh evaluation plan; these held-out trials do not justify tuning targets or intensity.
- **Reproduction / raw data.** `node tools/two_cue_transfer.mjs decode_two_bit_cue_gated_probe 21 02 lanes02 150`; [paired pathway measurements](../data/two_cue_decode_two_bit_cue_gated_probe_02_21_lanes02.json).

### Prospective direction after the cue-gating stop

| Approach | Limitation addressed and evidence | Small falsifier and controls | Neural model change |
|---|---|---|---|
| One-cue hidden-context transfer (selected) | Avoids failed simultaneous two-bit decoding. Fixed neuron-2132 bit at 1.915 separates mapping A/B on cue 1 in teachers 501–504 (trained bit-1 counts A: 34–36/36; B: 0–2/36); prior paired advantage was concentrated in mapping B/cue 1. | Fresh teacher 505 source gate, then one new student with matched correct, untrained, mapping-balanced shuffled, and disconnected training; all frozen students receive the same held-out correct messages. | None; task schedule uses cue 1 only and the established one-bit KC interface. |
| Blockwise strategy-switch transfer | Tests whether a pretrained teacher can shorten student adaptation rather than decode two simultaneous bits. Prior RPS teacher messages failed to help and source information was weak, so source information must be established first. | Fresh teacher source-information check against student observations and shuffled activity; stop before student training if no extra information. | None; task schedule changes. |
| Graded MBON population communication | Avoids threshold information loss. Four-MBON continuous activity contained held-out mapping information, but current receiving channel failed joint decoding. | Cue-free known-signal neural probe with fixed encoding versus disconnected/shuffled; reject if it cannot preserve cue processing and decoding. | No core-model change, but requires a new engineered communication interface; defer. |

**Predeclared gate for the selected one-cue pilot.** Fresh teacher 505 must score at least 75% frozen on both mappings and its fixed neuron-2132 bit must classify mapping on cue 1 at least 75% over 24 balanced held-out samples. Student training uses only recordings from teachers 501/502; evaluation uses teacher 505 and fresh Fly B seed 701. A promising one-seed result requires correct-signal training to gain at least 4/24 over its own frozen-before score, beat each control-trained student by at least 3/24 when all receive identical correct held-out messages, and score at least 8/12 on each mapping. This is a pilot gate, not a teaching claim or a parameter search.

### One-cue hidden-context pilot

- **Hypothesis / experiment.** A single learned source bit can support both actions when simultaneous two-bit decoding is removed. Cue 1 was constant; hidden mappings A/B were balanced and interleaved. Student training used actual 120 ms neuron-2132 traces from independently trained teachers 501/502 at the fixed threshold 1.915, with the established one-bit KC channel and eight ordinary reward-learning episodes. Teacher 505 and Fly B 701 were fresh; teacher-505 test-2 recordings supplied 24 held-out rounds, disjoint from student-training recordings.
- **Source gate.** Teacher 505 frozen accuracy was 67/72 (93.1%) on mapping A and 65/72 (90.3%) on B. Its cue-1 bit classified mapping on 22/24 held-out samples (11/12 each); the mapping-balanced shuffled bit was 6/12 bit-1 on each mapping, and untrained activity was bit-1 on all 24.
- **Matched controls and result.** Fly B 701 scored 13/24 before correct-signal training and 22/24 afterward, with 11/12 on each mapping. Untrained-, mapping-balanced shuffled-, and disconnected-signal training from the same starting state scored 16/24 (11/5 by mapping), 7/24 (5/2), and 13/24 (11/2) when all received the *same correct held-out teacher messages*. The correct-trained student fell to 12/24 when disconnected or given shuffled messages at evaluation. The predeclared one-seed pilot gate passed.
- **Conclusion / next step.** This is a learned, message-dependent response to activity from trained connectome networks in a deliberately simplified one-cue task, not reliable two-cue teaching or permanent mapping knowledge. Replicate prospectively with new teacher and student seeds, including the same paired training and held-out controls; do not alter the channel based on this pilot.
- **Reproduce / raw data.** `node tools/two_cue_transfer.mjs source 505 02 lanes02 150`; `node tools/two_cue_transfer.mjs single_cue_transfer 201 02 lanes02 150`; `data/two_cue_source_02_505_lanes02.json`; `data/two_cue_single_cue_transfer_02_201_lanes02.json`.

### Prospective one-cue replication gate

Keep neuron 2132, threshold 1.915, one-bit KC targets, 8 training episodes, teachers 501/502 for training, and all four training conditions fixed. Use fresh evaluation teacher 506 with student 702 first; if its source and student gates pass, repeat with teacher 507 and student 703. Each teacher must score at least 75% frozen on each mapping and classify at least 18/24 balanced held-out cue-1 messages. Each student must gain at least 4/24 over its own frozen-before score, beat every control-trained state by at least 3/24 on identical correct held-out messages, and reach at least 8/12 on each mapping. Record connected, disconnected, and shuffled frozen evaluations; stop expansion if the first fresh pair fails. This replicates a one-cue result, not joint two-bit decoding.

### One-cue fresh-pair replication

- **Question / hypothesis.** Would the fixed one-cue teacher signal produce a student improvement meeting the pilot gate with a new evaluation teacher and student?
- **Configuration / controls.** Teacher 506 trained for 10 episodes per mapping. Student 702 used the unchanged neuron-2132 threshold (1.915), one-bit KC targets, eight 24-round reward-learning episodes, and teacher-501/502 training recordings. Correct, untrained, mapping-balanced shuffled, and disconnected training began from the same student weights. Every trained state was frozen and tested on the same balanced 24 rounds from teacher-506 test-2 recordings. The correct-trained state also received disconnected and shuffled evaluation messages. No core mechanism changed; the runner now derives the evaluation teacher seed from the student run seed, preserving the pilot's seed-201/teacher-505 pairing.
- **Measurements.** Teacher 506 scored 68/72 frozen on each mapping; its bit classified all 24 held-out mapping samples. Student 702 scored 21/24 frozen before training (11/12 and 10/12 by mapping), then 24/24 after correct-signal training (12/12 each). Untrained-, shuffled-, and disconnected-trained states scored 14/24, 12/24, and 16/24 on identical correct messages. The correct-trained state scored 12/24 with messages disconnected or shuffled at evaluation. The connected final score and controls support a learned, message-dependent response in this pair.
- **Result / limitations.** The predeclared replication gate **failed** because the within-student gain was only 3/24, below 4/24; a high frozen-before score left little room to improve. This is one fresh pair, and it does not establish reliable teaching or resolve joint two-bit decoding. Per the stop rule, teacher 507/student 703 were not run. Do not lower the gate after seeing this result or retune the channel on these held-out rounds.
- **Next experiment.** No further experiment on this locked replication line is justified within this session. A future study would need a separately predeclared question and fresh evaluation cohorts.
- **Reproduce / raw data.** `node tools/two_cue_transfer.mjs source 506 02 lanes02 150`; `node tools/two_cue_transfer.mjs single_cue_transfer 202 02 lanes02 150`; [teacher recordings](../data/two_cue_source_02_506_lanes02.json); [student and controls](../data/two_cue_single_cue_transfer_02_202_lanes02.json).

### Frozen initial-state message control for student 702

- **Question / hypothesis.** Did student 702's 21/24 pretraining score reflect an immediate response to informative teacher stimulation rather than reward learning? With all initial synaptic weights frozen, correct teacher bits should outperform mapping-balanced shuffled, untrained-teacher, and disconnected messages on the same 24-round held-out schedule.
- **Configuration / controls.** Replayed student seed 702 with evaluation teacher 506's saved test-2 cue-1 bits, the unchanged 2132 threshold (1.915), KC targets, and message/cue timing. Four evaluations differed only in message source: correct trained, mapping-balanced shuffled, untrained teacher, or disconnected. No training episodes, dopamine feedback, or weight updates occurred. Teacher 506 had previously passed the source gate (68/72 frozen per mapping and 24/24 held-out bits).
- **Measurements.** Frozen initial scores were correct **21/24** (mapping A/B: 11/12, 10/12), shuffled **14/24** (12/12, 2/12), untrained **12/24** (12/12, 0/12), and disconnected **12/24** (12/12, 0/12). The correct and disconnected replays exactly matched the archived before-training results. The correct signal exceeded every control by at least 7/24, concentrated in mapping B. Correct bits were balanced perfectly by mapping (12 bit-1 for A, 0 for B); shuffled bits were 6/12 bit-1 in each mapping.
- **Result / limits.** The high 21/24 pretraining score is largely an immediate, mapping-dependent response to stimulation in this student; it cannot be counted as learning. Training still raised this student's correct-message score from 21/24 to 24/24, and its trained state beat matched control-trained states on correct messages, but the fixed 4/24 gain replication gate remains failed. These reused held-out recordings and one student do not establish the prevalence of the baseline effect or a general teaching result. The locked one-cue replication line remains stopped.
- **Next justified question.** In an independent baseline-mechanism diagnostic, does variation in initial message-evoked action-0 MBON drive explain why student 702 starts at 10/12 on mapping B while student 701 started at 2/12? Compare frozen neural responses at the same teacher-506 message schedule across the two initial student states, with no training. A higher action-0 message/decision response in 702 on mapping-B trials would support the mechanism; a similar response despite the choice gap would reject it. This is a new measurement-validity question, not a new teaching gate.
- **Reproduce / raw data.** `node tools/two_cue_transfer.mjs single_cue_baseline_probe 202 02 lanes02 150`; [frozen initial-state controls](../data/two_cue_single_cue_baseline_probe_02_202_lanes02.json); [archived replication](../data/two_cue_single_cue_transfer_02_202_lanes02.json).

### Initial message-drive comparison on one shared schedule

- **Question / hypothesis.** Does stronger initial message-evoked action-0 MBON drive explain student 702's higher mapping-B baseline than student 701's? The prospective diagnostic expected higher message-window and decision action-0 rates in 702 on the same teacher-506 held-out rounds.
- **Experiment / controls.** Reused teacher 506's 24 balanced held-out cue-1 bits and the exact seed-202 schedule. Replayed initial, untrained students 701 and 702 with correct trained messages or disconnected input; weights stayed frozen, and neither run received feedback. The established 2132 threshold, one-bit KC targets, cue timing, and MBON readout were unchanged. Measured per-action MBON rates after the 0–40 ms message and at the 240 ms decision. This was one mechanism diagnostic, not a teaching replication.
- **Measurements.** With correct messages, student 701 scored 15/24 (mapping A/B: 12/12, 3/12); student 702 scored 21/24 (11/12, 10/12). Disconnected scores were 11/24 (11/12, 0/12) and 12/24 (12/12, 0/12). On the 12 mapping-B trials, correct-message action-0 rates after the message were 0.173 versus 0.185 spikes per MBON for 701 versus 702; decision action-0 rates were 0.232 versus 0.220. Correct-message action-2 decision rates instead were 0.381 versus 0.089. Against disconnected trials, correct messages reduced action-2 decision rates by 0.018 in 701 and 0.375 in 702; they raised action-0 rates by 0.208 and 0.196. Message-window action-2 rates were 0.054 and 0.012 with correct messages. Student 702's correct/disconnected scores exactly match the archived frozen baseline.
- **Conclusion / limits.** The proposed stronger action-0 message-drive mechanism failed: action-0 rates were similar despite a 7/12 mapping-B choice gap. The differing action-2 decision response is a new clue, but its cause is unresolved. These are two reused student seeds, one teacher, and one trial schedule; trial-to-trial neural state or a within-trial message/cue interaction could contribute. No learning or general teaching claim follows, and the locked 4/24 replication gain remains failed.
- **Next distinct diagnostic.** Hypothesis: the student-specific action-2 suppression is generated within a message-plus-cue trial rather than inherited from preceding rounds. In a future frozen replay, reset neural state before every held-out round for both students and compare correct versus disconnected messages on each mapping-B round. If 702 still shows a larger action-2 decision-rate reduction than 701, the within-trial explanation survives; if the difference disappears, prior-round state is implicated. Do not run another teaching gate based on this diagnostic alone.
- **Reproduce / raw data.** `node tools/two_cue_transfer.mjs single_cue_initial_drive 202 02 lanes02 150`; [per-round neural rates](../data/two_cue_single_cue_initial_drive_02_202_lanes02.json).

### Round-start neural-state reset at the frozen one-cue baseline

- **Question / hypothesis.** Does student 702's larger message-associated action-2 suppression arise within each message-plus-cue trial, or from neural activity carried between rounds? If within-trial, the 702-versus-701 suppression gap should persist when the network state is reset before every held-out round.
- **Configuration / controls.** Reused teacher 506's 24 balanced, held-out cue-1 bits and the fixed seed-202 schedule. Initial, untrained students 701 and 702 each received correct trained messages or disconnected input at frozen weights. The only new intervention was `net.resetState()` at every round start rather than only at episode start; source bits, stimulation, cue, reward labels, and readout stayed fixed. No feedback, dopamine learning, or weight updates occurred. The archived continuous-round replay is the comparison control. Student seeds: 701/702; teacher seed: 506; training budget: zero.
- **Measurements.** In the archived continuous replay, mapping-B correct-message scores were 3/12 (701) and 10/12 (702), with disconnected scores 0/12 each. Correct messages reduced mean action-2 decision rates relative to disconnection by 0.018 and 0.375 spikes per MBON, respectively. With round resets, correct-message mapping-B scores were **11/12 and 10/12**, versus 0/12 disconnected in both; action-2 reductions were **0.036 and 0.018**. Correct-message action-2 rates themselves fell from 0.381 to 0.042 in 701 and 0.089 to 0.018 in 702. Across all 24 rounds, reset scores were 20/24 and 19/24 correct-message versus 2/24 and 3/24 disconnected. Message-window action-0 rates on mapping B remained similar (0.155 and 0.137).
- **Result / limitations.** The predicted larger 702 action-2 suppression did not survive round resets, and the 701/702 mapping-B choice gap disappeared. Prior-round neural state therefore materially affects the earlier baseline comparison. Resetting the entire state also changes the cue-only/disconnected baseline and time alignment, so this does not identify a particular state variable or prove that any specific previous message caused the effect. It is a measurement diagnostic, not a teaching or learning replication; the locked 4/24 gain gate remains failed.
- **Next justified experiment.** Test a specific carryover source at frozen weights: prepend either a bit-0 or bit-1 teacher message in an otherwise identical immediately preceding trial, reset only before that two-trial pair, then compare the same current mapping-B bit-0 probe in each student. Predeclare 12 paired probes per student. The message-history hypothesis passes only if preceding bit 1 raises student 701's mean current action-2 rate by at least 0.10 spikes per MBON and costs at least 3/12 action-0 choices versus preceding bit 0; otherwise reject this specific explanation. Keep cue, previous-trial reward handling, current bit, and weights fixed. Do not rerun the stopped teaching gate based on this diagnostic.
- **Reproduce / raw data.** `node tools/two_cue_transfer.mjs single_cue_round_reset 202 02 lanes02 150`; [round-reset per-round rates](../data/two_cue_single_cue_round_reset_02_202_lanes02.json); [continuous-round comparison](../data/two_cue_single_cue_initial_drive_02_202_lanes02.json).

### Frozen preceding-bit pair diagnostic

- **Question / hypothesis.** Does a bit-1 message in the immediately preceding trial leave action-2 activity that explains student 701's low continuous-round mapping-B baseline? The prospective gate required at least +0.10 spikes per MBON in the current action-2 decision rate and at least 3/12 fewer current action-0 choices after preceding bit 1 versus bit 0 in student 701.
- **Experiment / controls.** Used teacher 506's 12 held-out mapping-B bit-0 samples as current probes. For each untrained student 701/702, ran 12 frozen two-trial pairs with either bit 0 or bit 1 in the preceding trial; current bit was always 0. Both trials kept cue 1, original 300 Hz/40 ms one-bit KC channel, 70–240 ms cue, no feedback, and the original readout. Neural state was reset only before each pair. Each arm used the same 12 current source rounds; weights were checked unchanged. Training budget: zero. Student 702 served as a parallel baseline comparison, not another teaching seed.
- **Measurements.** Student 701: preceding bit 0 yielded 11/12 current action-0 choices and mean current action-2 rate 0.018 spikes per MBON; preceding bit 1 yielded 10/12 and 0.018. Thus the changes were −1/12 choices and **0.000** rate, below both fixed gates. Student 702: 10/12 and 0.060 after bit 0, versus 9/12 and 0.089 after bit 1 (−1/12 and +0.030). No reward or synaptic change occurred.
- **Conclusion / limits.** The specific immediately preceding bit-1 explanation failed. This does not rule out longer trial history or stochastic schedule effects. The full-reset intervention changed several neural state variables and did not restart the random stream, so its large baseline change cannot be assigned uniquely to a particular carried signal. The locked one-cue replication gate remains failed; no teaching run followed.
- **Next direction.** Test baseline measurement stability across trial order, without another message-history intervention. Reorder the same 24 held-out teacher-506 bits and mapping labels into two independently fixed, balanced schedules and replay frozen students 701/702 with no feedback. The student-specific baseline gap passes a prospective robustness gate only if 702 exceeds 701 by at least 4/12 mapping-B correct choices on **both** new schedules; otherwise treat the prior 7/12 gap as order-sensitive. This is a separate diagnostic for a later session, not a renewed teaching gate.
- **Reproduce / raw data.** `node tools/two_cue_transfer.mjs single_cue_preceding_bit 202 02 lanes02 150`; [per-pair current choices and rates](../data/two_cue_single_cue_preceding_bit_02_202_lanes02.json); [prior full-reset comparison](../data/two_cue_single_cue_round_reset_02_202_lanes02.json).

### Frozen held-out order robustness diagnostic

- **Question / hypothesis.** Would student 702's original 7/12 mapping-B pretraining advantage over student 701 persist when the same held-out teacher-506 trials were reordered? The prospective gate required 702 to exceed 701 by at least 4/12 on both new orders.
- **Experiment / controls.** Permuted the same 24 teacher-506 test-2 trial tuples (mapping label, recorded neuron-2132 bit, source-round identity) with two fixed shuffle seeds, 20261103 and 20261104. Each order remained 12/12 mapping-balanced with 12 bit-1 and 12 bit-0 messages. Replayed initial student seeds 701/702 with frozen weights, either correct trained messages or no message. The established 300 Hz, 0–40 ms KC channel, 70–240 ms cue, readout, and episode-start state reset were unchanged. No feedback or training occurred.
- **Measurements.** Original-order mapping-B correct-message scores were 3/12 (701) and 10/12 (702). New order 20261103 yielded 4/12 (701) and 3/12 (702); order 20261104 yielded 10/12 (701) and 4/12 (702). Total correct-message scores were 16/24 versus 14/24, then 19/24 versus 16/24. Disconnected mapping-B scores were 0/12 in both students on both orders; disconnected totals stayed 11/24 (701) and 12/24 (702).
- **Result / limits.** The predeclared robustness gate failed on both new orders, with the direction of the student gap reversed. The original 7/12 gap is order-sensitive and cannot support a stable student-specific baseline advantage. Correct teacher stimulation can still affect immediate choices, but this frozen diagnostic does not measure learning. The order effect could reflect trial-to-trial neural carryover, random-stream alignment, or their interaction; these replays do not separate them. The one-cue teaching replication gate remains failed.
- **Next justified experiment.** Test whether carried neural state accounts for the order sensitivity by replaying these exact two fixed orders with `resetEachRound`, keeping message tuples and frozen weights fixed. A prospective carryover-consistent gate is that student 701's mapping-B score difference between orders shrinks from 6/12 to at most 2/12; otherwise reject this specific explanation. Do not reopen the stopped teaching gate.
- **Reproduce / raw data.** `node tools/two_cue_transfer.mjs single_cue_order_probe 202 02 lanes02 150`; [two frozen orders and controls](../data/two_cue_single_cue_order_probe_02_202_lanes02.json). An initial dispatch error generated an unrelated simulation and was retained separately as `data/two_cue_single_cue_order_probe_02_202_lanes02_invalid_dispatch.json`; it was excluded from analysis.

### Frozen held-out order reset diagnostic

- **Question / hypothesis.** Does carried neural state contribute to the order-sensitive initial mapping-B score? The prospective gate required student 701's difference between the two fixed orders to shrink from 6/12 to at most 2/12 when state is reset before each trial.
- **Experiment / controls.** Replayed the exact teacher-506 test-2 tuples in fixed orders 20261103 and 20261104 with frozen initial students 701/702. The only intervention was `resetEachRound`; the same teacher bits, mapping labels, source-round identities, 300 Hz/40 ms KC channel, cue timing, weights, and readout were used. Each student also received disconnected replays. No reward or training occurred. Saved schedules were checked against the prior continuous replay.
- **Measurements.** Student 701 mapping-B correct-message scores changed from 4/12 and 10/12 in the continuous replays to **11/12 and 9/12** with per-round reset, reducing the gap from 6/12 to 2/12. Its total scores changed from 16/24 and 19/24 to 22/24 and 17/24. Student 702 mapping-B scores changed from 3/12 and 4/12 to 6/12 and 9/12. Disconnected totals fell sharply: 701 from 11/24 on both orders to 2/24 and 3/24; 702 from 12/24 on both to 2/24 on both. Disconnected mapping-B scores were 0/12 except 701 on the second reset order (1/12).
- **Result / limitations.** The predeclared carryover-consistent gate passed, so prior trial history contributes to the baseline measurement. `resetState()` clears voltage, conductance, refractory state, trace, spike counts, delayed inputs, and drive, while leaving the random generator running. State resets can alter later random-number consumption, and the large disconnected-control shift shows that this intervention changes baseline behavior beyond the connected score. It does not isolate a particular carried neural variable or demonstrate learning. The locked one-cue teaching replication gate remains failed.
- **Next justified experiment.** Separate carried-state effects from random-stream alignment on these same fixed orders: leave neural state continuous but assign each source trial a fixed, order-independent random stream at its start. Prospectively, a student-701 mapping-B order gap of at most 2/12 supports random-stream alignment as a major contributor; a gap of at least 3/12 fails that criterion. Do not reopen the teaching gate.
- **Reproduce / raw data.** `node tools/two_cue_transfer.mjs single_cue_order_reset 202 02 lanes02 150`; [reset results](../data/two_cue_single_cue_order_reset_02_202_lanes02.json); [continuous comparison](../data/two_cue_single_cue_order_probe_02_202_lanes02.json).

### Frozen held-out order with fixed trial random streams

- **Question / hypothesis.** Did order-dependent random-stream alignment account for student 701's mapping-B score difference across the two fixed held-out orders? The prospective gate was a gap of at most 2/12 with each source trial assigned the same random stream in either order; 3/12 or more would fail.
- **Experiment / controls.** Replayed the same 24 teacher-506 held-out trial tuples in orders 20261103 and 20261104 for frozen, untrained students 701/702. Neural state remained continuous between rounds. At each round start, the network Poisson and agent action-tie random generators were seeded from student ID and the trial's original held-out index, independent of order. Correct trained messages and disconnected controls used the same trial seeds within each order. Cue timing, channel, weights, and reward-free evaluation were unchanged; training budget was zero. The runner checked weights unchanged.
- **Measurements.** Student 701 mapping-B correct-message scores were **9/12 and 3/12** (gap 6/12), versus 4/12 and 10/12 (gap 6/12) in the prior continuously advancing random-stream replay. Its total scores were 18/24 and 15/24; disconnected totals were 3/24 and 3/24. Student 702 mapping-B scores were 8/12 and 2/12; total scores were 20/24 and 14/24, with disconnected totals 10/24 and 8/24.
- **Result / limitations.** The fixed random-stream gate failed. Random-stream alignment alone does not explain away the order sensitivity under this intervention. Replacing the random streams changed absolute scores and disconnected behavior, so these data do not quantify a pure neural-state effect or identify which state variable carries history. An initial replay fixed only network randomness and left agent tie breaks order-dependent; its output is preserved but excluded from this conclusion. The original one-cue 4/24 gain replication gate remains failed; this frozen diagnostic is not a learning or teaching test.
- **Next justified experiment.** On the *same fixed trial streams and orders*, change only the round-start neural-state reset. If student 701's 6/12 mapping-B order gap falls to at most 2/12, that supports a carried-state contribution under controlled trial randomness; if it remains at least 3/12, this specific state explanation fails. Keep the teaching line stopped.
- **Reproduce / raw data.** `node tools/two_cue_transfer.mjs single_cue_order_rng_full 202 02 lanes02 150`; [corrected fixed-stream results](../data/two_cue_single_cue_order_rng_full_02_202_lanes02.json); [incomplete network-only replay](../data/two_cue_single_cue_order_rng_02_202_lanes02.json); [original order replay](../data/two_cue_single_cue_order_probe_02_202_lanes02.json).

### Frozen held-out order with fixed streams and round resets

- **Question / hypothesis.** Does state carried between rounds explain the mapping-B order gap that remained after fixing each trial's random streams? The prospective gate required student 701's 6/12 gap to fall to at most 2/12 after a round-start `net.resetState()`; 3/12 or more would fail.
- **Experiment / controls.** Replayed teacher 506's same 24 held-out cue-1 tuples in orders 20261103 and 20261104 for initial student seeds 701/702. Network and action-tie random streams were fixed by student and original source-trial index exactly as in the archived continuous-state run. Only round-start neural-state reset changed. Both correct trained messages and disconnected controls were evaluated with frozen weights, no feedback, and zero training episodes. The saved indices and bits matched the earlier run, and each source trial's action was identical across the two reset orders within each student and message condition.
- **Measurements.** Student 701 mapping-B correct-message scores changed from 9/12 and 3/12 with continuous state to **5/12 and 5/12** with resets; total scores were 13/24 on both orders. Student 702 changed from 8/12 and 2/12 to **9/12 and 9/12**, with 20/24 total on both orders. Disconnected totals with resets were 3/24 on both orders for each student; mapping-B disconnected scores were 1/12 on both orders. Correct messages still exceeded disconnection, but that is an immediate frozen response, not learning.
- **Result / limitations.** The predeclared gap gate passed: the order difference vanished for student 701 under matched trial streams and round resets. This identifies some round-history dependence under this diagnostic but does not identify which reset variable matters. `resetState()` also restarts network time and clears voltage, conductance, refractory state, traces, delayed inputs, counts, and drive; absolute scores and disconnected behavior changed relative to continuous replay, especially for student 702. The locked one-cue teaching replication remains failed, and no teaching run followed.
- **Next justified diagnostic.** Test whether residual action-2 MBON conductance at the start of a trial predicts the order-dependent mapping-B choice in the archived fixed-stream continuous protocol. For student 701, compare the same source trials in both orders at frozen weights; among trials with discordant correct-message choices, the hypothesis passes only if at least 75% have higher initial mean action-2 MBON conductance in the order that chooses action 2, and the mean conductance difference has the same sign. Otherwise reject this specific carried-state mechanism. Include the disconnected replay as a baseline and do not reopen the teaching gate.
- **Reproduce / raw data.** `node tools/two_cue_transfer.mjs single_cue_order_rng_reset 202 02 lanes02 150`; [round-reset fixed-stream results](../data/two_cue_single_cue_order_rng_reset_02_202_lanes02.json); [continuous fixed-stream comparison](../data/two_cue_single_cue_order_rng_full_02_202_lanes02.json).

### Trial-start action-2 MBON conductance diagnostic

- **Question / hypothesis.** Does residual action-2 MBON conductance at trial start predict the mapping-B errors that differ between the two fixed held-out orders? The prospective gate required higher mean starting conductance in the action-2 order for at least 75% of student-701 discordant trials, with a positive mean paired difference.
- **Experiment / controls.** Replayed the archived teacher-506 cue-1 tuples in orders 20261103 and 20261104 for frozen initial students 701/702, with the same per-source-trial network and action-tie random streams and continuous neural state. Recorded mean signed `net.g` over action-2 MBONs at each round start, before stimulation. Correct trained messages and disconnected replays were run; no feedback or learning occurred. Every action in all eight student/order/message combinations exactly matched the archived fixed-stream run. Training budget: zero; only the observation was added.
- **Measurements.** Student 701 reproduced 9/12 and 3/12 mapping-B correct-message scores across the orders (18/24 and 15/24 overall); student 702 reproduced 8/12 and 2/12 (20/24 and 14/24 overall). Disconnected totals reproduced 3/24 and 3/24 for 701, 10/24 and 8/24 for 702. Among eight student-701 mapping-B source trials with discordant choices and an action-2 choice in one order, starting action-2 conductance was higher in that order for **5/8 (62.5%)**, below the fixed 75% gate. Mean paired difference was +0.316 conductance units, but disconnected differences were positive in only 4/8. Seven discordances were action 0 versus 2; one was action 1 versus 2.
- **Result / limitations.** The specified conductance-prediction hypothesis failed its gate. The positive mean does not rescue the failed directional criterion. Aggregate conductance is only one component of neural state; this passive measurement does not rule out voltage, refractory state, delayed input, or distributed network history. Reused teacher/student seeds and held-out orders limit generalization. This is not a learning test, and the prior one-cue replication gate remains failed.
- **Next distinct question.** Trial-start MBON membrane voltage is a separate carried-state candidate. On the same frozen fixed-stream replays, test whether student-701 discordant mapping-B trials have higher mean action-2 MBON voltage in the order choosing action 2 in at least 75% of cases, with a positive paired mean; otherwise reject that candidate. Do not alter the failed conductance gate or resume teaching from this diagnostic alone.
- **Reproduce / raw data.** `node tools/two_cue_transfer.mjs single_cue_order_conductance 202 02 lanes02 150`; [trial-start conductance and paired choices](../data/two_cue_single_cue_order_conductance_02_202_lanes02.json); [archived fixed-stream control](../data/two_cue_single_cue_order_rng_full_02_202_lanes02.json).

### Trial-start action-2 MBON voltage diagnostic

- **Question / hypothesis.** Does higher mean action-2 MBON membrane voltage at trial start predict the mapping-B action-2 choices that differ between the two fixed orders? The prospective gate required a higher voltage in the action-2 order for at least 75% of student-701 discordant trials and a positive mean paired difference.
- **Experiment / controls.** Replayed the same teacher-506 held-out cue-1 tuples in orders 20261103 and 20261104 for frozen initial students 701/702. Network and action-tie random streams were fixed by source-trial identity; neural state remained continuous. Measured mean `net.v` over action-2 MBONs before each round's stimulation, with correct trained messages and disconnected controls. No feedback or training occurred. All eight student/order/message action sequences matched the archived fixed-stream replay; training budget was zero.
- **Measurements.** Student 701's mapping-B correct-message scores remained 9/12 and 3/12 (18/24 and 15/24 overall); student 702's remained 8/12 and 2/12 (20/24 and 14/24 overall). Disconnected totals remained 3/24 and 3/24 for 701, and 10/24 and 8/24 for 702. Among the eight student-701 mapping-B trials with discordant choices involving action 2, starting action-2 voltage was higher in the action-2 order on **0/8**; mean paired action-2-minus-other-order difference was **−0.358 mV**. Disconnected paired differences had mixed signs (4/8 positive).
- **Result / limitations.** The predeclared higher-voltage gate failed; its direction cannot be reversed after seeing the result. The uniformly lower voltages are an observation, not evidence that voltage causes the choices. Membrane voltage may reflect preceding spikes, refractory state, or other carried activity. Reused seeds and orders limit inference, and this frozen diagnostic provides no learning or teaching evidence. The one-cue replication gate remains failed.
- **Next distinct question.** Does a higher fraction of action-2 MBONs in refractory state at trial start accompany the lower voltages in the action-2 order? A passive replay on the same fixed streams and orders would pass this *new* mechanism diagnostic only if at least 6/8 student-701 discordant trials have a higher refractory fraction in the action-2 order and the mean paired difference is positive. Include disconnected replays and require archived action agreement. This would test whether the inverse voltage pattern marks recent spiking; it would not reopen the teaching gate.
- **Reproduce / raw data.** `node tools/two_cue_transfer.mjs single_cue_order_voltage 202 02 lanes02 150`; [trial-start voltage and paired choices](../data/two_cue_single_cue_order_voltage_02_202_lanes02.json); [archived fixed-stream control](../data/two_cue_single_cue_order_rng_full_02_202_lanes02.json).

### Trial-start action-2 MBON refractory diagnostic

- **Question / hypothesis.** Does a larger fraction of action-2 MBONs in refractory state at trial start accompany the lower voltages and order-dependent action-2 choices? The prospective gate was a higher fraction in the action-2 order for at least 6/8 student-701 discordant mapping-B trials, with a positive mean paired difference.
- **Experiment / controls.** Replayed teacher 506's held-out cue-1 trials in the same fixed orders 20261103/20261104 for frozen initial students 701/702. Measured the fraction of action-2 MBONs with `net.refr > 0` before each trial, with correct trained messages and disconnected controls. Source-trial network and action-tie streams stayed fixed; neural state remained continuous. No feedback or training occurred. The runner verified unchanged weights and exact agreement with every archived action.
- **Measurements.** Student 701's mapping-B correct-message scores remained 9/12 and 3/12; student 702's remained 8/12 and 2/12. Student-701 disconnected totals remained 3/24 in both orders; student-702 disconnected totals remained 10/24 and 8/24. The refractory fraction was higher in the action-2 order on **0/8** discordant student-701 trials; mean paired difference was **0**. It was zero on all 48 student-701 correct-message round starts and 47/48 student-701 disconnected starts.
- **Conclusion / limits.** The predeclared refractory gate failed. Trial-start action-2 refractoriness does not account for these order-dependent errors. This passive measure cannot exclude other distributed or delayed state, but three candidate readouts (conductance, voltage, refractory fraction) have failed to identify a usable mechanism on the same reused trials. The one-cue replication gate remains failed; this is not evidence of learning.
- **Next direction.** End the individual state-variable search. A distinct measurement-validity question is whether a persistent reward-driven student change can be separated from immediate stimulation when each frozen evaluation trial starts from a reset state and receives the same source-specific random stream. Compare the archived pre/post student states and message-source training controls on the same teacher-506 schedule. Prospectively require at least 4/24 gain for the correct-trained student under this fixed evaluation protocol, plus at least 4/24 advantage over each control-trained student; otherwise this route does not support learning. This is a new diagnostic, not a revision of the failed original gate, and belongs to a later session.
- **Reproduce / raw data.** `node tools/two_cue_transfer.mjs single_cue_order_refractory 202 02 lanes02 150`; [paired refractory measurements](../data/two_cue_single_cue_order_refractory_02_202_lanes02.json); [archived fixed-stream choices](../data/two_cue_single_cue_order_rng_full_02_202_lanes02.json).

### Fixed-stream, round-reset learning-control diagnostic

- **Research question / hypothesis.** Does reward training with correct teacher activity yield a persistent student advantage beyond immediate stimulation? The prospective gate required a gain of at least 4/24 from the frozen initial state and an advantage of at least 4/24 over each untrained-teacher, mapping-balanced shuffled, and disconnected training control when all receive correct held-out teacher bits.
- **Experiment / controls.** Reconstructed student 702's four archived eight-episode, 24-round training conditions using teacher recordings 501/502 and the unchanged 2132 one-bit KC channel. The runner verified the held-out/training plans, every archived training score, and each archived continuous-evaluation score. It then evaluated frozen initial and trained weights on teacher 506's same 24 balanced held-out trials, resetting neural state before each round and fixing network and action-tie random streams by original source-trial index. All control-trained students received the same correct messages at evaluation; the correctly trained student was also evaluated with disconnected and shuffled messages. No learning occurred during evaluation, and frozen weights were checked unchanged. One student seed, 702; training budget was the archived eight episodes per condition.
- **Measurements.** Correct-message scores: initial **20/24** (mapping A/B 11/12, 9/12); correctly trained **24/24** (12/12, 12/12); untrained-teacher-trained **18/24**; shuffled-trained **13/24**; disconnected-trained **24/24**. The correct-trained gain was **+4/24**, and advantages over the three training controls were +6/24, +11/24, and **0/24**. The correctly trained state scored 12/24 when evaluation messages were disconnected and 12/24 when shuffled.
- **Result / limitations.** The gain criterion passed, but the disconnected-training control tied the correct-trained state, so the prospective joint gate **failed**. Correct evaluation messages are necessary for the correctly trained state's perfect score under this protocol, yet a student trained with no teacher messages can also reach 24/24 when given those messages later. This diagnostic does not distinguish teacher-specific reward learning from generic reward adaptation combined with immediate stimulation. It reuses one student, teacher cohorts, and held-out messages; resetting state changes the evaluation protocol and does not revise the failed original replication gate. Stop this learning-control line without retuning it.
- **Next distinct question.** Does the disconnected-trained student's 24/24 depend on immediate informative stimulation? At its frozen trained weights, evaluate the same fixed-stream, round-reset schedule with correct, mapping-balanced shuffled, and disconnected messages. Prospectively, the message-dependence hypothesis passes only if **both** control-message scores are at least 4/24 below its 24/24 correct-message score; otherwise reject it. This would explain the control tie, not establish teaching, and belongs to a later session.
- **Reproduce / raw data.** `node tools/two_cue_transfer.mjs single_cue_learning_reset 202 02 lanes02 150`; [fixed-evaluation measurements](../data/two_cue_single_cue_learning_reset_02_202_lanes02.json); [archived training/control run](../data/two_cue_single_cue_transfer_02_202_lanes02.json).
